### Q1. JEE Main 2026 (08 April Shift 2)

*Type:* `singleCorrect` | *Level:* `2`

$K_1$ and $K_2$ be the maximum kinetic energies of photoelectrons emitted from a surface of a given material for the light of wavelength $\lambda_1$ and $\lambda_2$, respectively. If $\lambda_1=2\lambda_2$ then the work function of material is given by:

**Options:**

- **(A)** $K_2+2K_1$
- **(B)** $2K_2-K_1$
- **(C)** $K_1-2K_2$
- **(D)** $K_2-2K_1$

<details>
<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Correct Option:** **(D) $K_2-2K_1$**

**Detailed Solution:**

Using Einstein's photoelectric equation:

$K_1 = \dfrac{hc}{\lambda_1} - \phi$

$K_2 = \dfrac{hc}{\lambda_2} - \phi$

Given $\lambda_1 = 2\lambda_2$, the first equation becomes:

$K_1 = \dfrac{hc}{2\lambda_2} - \phi$

Multiplying by $2$:

$2K_1 = \dfrac{hc}{\lambda_2} - 2\phi$

Substituting $\dfrac{hc}{\lambda_2} = K_2 + \phi$ from the second equation:

$2K_1 = (K_2 + \phi) - 2\phi$

$2K_1 = K_2 - \phi$

$\phi = K_2 - 2K_1$

Answer: $K_2-2K_1$

</details>

### Q2. JEE Main 2026 (05 April Shift 2)

*Type:* `singleCorrect` | *Level:* `2`

Given below are two statements: one is labelled as Assertion (A) and the other is labelled as Reason (R).
Assertion (A): The electromagnetic wave exerts pressure on the surface on which they are allowed to fall.
Reason (R): There is no mass associated with the electromagnetic waves.
In the light of the above statements, choose the correct answer from the options given below :

**Options:**

- **(A)** Both (A) and (R) are true and (R) is the correct explanation of (A)
- **(B)** Both (A) and (R) are true but (R) is not the correct explanation of (A)
- **(C)** (A) is true but (R) is false
- **(D)** (A) is false but (R) is true

<details>
<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Correct Option:** **(B) Both (A) and (R) are true but (R) is not the correct explanation of (A)**

**Detailed Solution:**

Assertion (A) is true because electromagnetic waves carry energy and momentum. When they strike a surface, they transfer momentum to it, which results in a force and consequently exerts radiation pressure on the surface.

Reason (R) is true because electromagnetic waves consist of photons, which have zero rest mass. Thus, there is no rest mass associated with electromagnetic waves.

However, the pressure exerted by electromagnetic waves is due to the momentum they carry ($p = \dfrac{E}{c}$), not because they are massless. Therefore, both (A) and (R) are true, but (R) is not the correct explanation of (A).

Answer: Both (A) and (R) are true but (R) is not the correct explanation of (A)

</details>

### Q3. JEE Main 2026 (05 April Shift 1)

*Type:* `singleCorrect` | *Level:* `2`

Light source having wavelength $331$ nm is used to generate photo-electrons whose stopping potential is $0.2$ V. The work function of the used metal in the experiment is $\alpha \times 10^{-19}$ J. The value of $\alpha$ is _____.
($h = 6.62 \times 10^{-34}$ J s, $e = 1.6 \times 10^{-19}$ C and $c = 3 \times 10^8$ m/s)

**Options:**

- **(A)** $3.68$
- **(B)** $4.68$
- **(C)** $5.68$
- **(D)** $2.68$

<details>
<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Correct Option:** **(C) $5.68$**

**Detailed Solution:**

Using Einstein's photoelectric equation:

$\dfrac{hc}{\lambda} = \Phi + eV_0$

The energy of the incident photon is:

$E = \dfrac{hc}{\lambda} = \dfrac{6.62 \times 10^{-34} \times 3 \times 10^8}{331 \times 10^{-9}}$

$E = \dfrac{19.86 \times 10^{-26}}{331 \times 10^{-9}} = 6 \times 10^{-19}$ J

The maximum kinetic energy of the photoelectrons is:

$K_{\text{max}} = eV_0 = 1.6 \times 10^{-19} \times 0.2 = 0.32 \times 10^{-19}$ J

The work function $\Phi$ is:

$\Phi = E - K_{\text{max}}$

$\Phi = 6 \times 10^{-19} - 0.32 \times 10^{-19} = 5.68 \times 10^{-19}$ J

Comparing with $\alpha \times 10^{-19}$ J, we get $\alpha = 5.68$.

Answer: $5.68$

</details>

### Q4. JEE Main 2026 (04 April Shift 2)

*Type:* `singleCorrect` | *Level:* `2`

Match the LIST-I with LIST-II
<table style="border: 1px solid black; border-collapse: collapse; width: auto;"><tr><th style="border: 1px solid black; padding: 5px;">List-I</th><th style="border: 1px solid black; padding: 5px;">List-II</th></tr><tr><td style="border: 1px solid black; padding: 5px;">A. Planck's constant</td><td style="border: 1px solid black; padding: 5px;">I. $ML^2T^{-2}$</td></tr><tr><td style="border: 1px solid black; padding: 5px;">B. Stopping potential</td><td style="border: 1px solid black; padding: 5px;">II. $T^{-1}$</td></tr><tr><td style="border: 1px solid black; padding: 5px;">C. Work function</td><td style="border: 1px solid black; padding: 5px;">III. $ML^2T^{-1}$</td></tr><tr><td style="border: 1px solid black; padding: 5px;">D. Threshold frequency</td><td style="border: 1px solid black; padding: 5px;">IV. $ML^2T^{-3}A^{-1}$</td></tr></table>
Choose the correct answer from the options given below:

**Options:**

- **(A)** A-III, B-IV, C-I, D-II
- **(B)** A-I, B-II, C-III, D-IV
- **(C)** A-IV, B-III, C-I, D-II
- **(D)** A-I, B-IV, C-III, D-II

<details>
<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Correct Option:** **(A) A-III, B-IV, C-I, D-II**

**Detailed Solution:**

The dimensional formula for Planck's constant $h$ is obtained from $E = h\nu$.

$[h] = \dfrac{[E]}{[\nu]} = \dfrac{ML^2T^{-2}}{T^{-1}} = ML^2T^{-1}$

The dimensional formula for stopping potential $V_0$ is obtained from $eV_0 = E$.

$[V_0] = \dfrac{[E]}{[e]} = \dfrac{ML^2T^{-2}}{AT} = ML^2T^{-3}A^{-1}$

Work function is a form of energy, so its dimensional formula is the same as that of energy.

$[\text{Work function}] = ML^2T^{-2}$

Threshold frequency has the dimensional formula of frequency.

$[\text{Threshold frequency}] = T^{-1}$

Therefore, the correct matching is A-III, B-IV, C-I, D-II.

Answer: A-III, B-IV, C-I, D-II

</details>

### Q5. JEE Main 2026 (04 April Shift 1)

*Type:* `singleCorrect` | *Level:* `2`

The graph shows variation of stopping potential $V_o$ with the frequency $\nu$ of the incident radiation for three photosensitive metals $X_1$, $X_2$ and $X_3$. Which metal will give out electrons with greater kinetic energy, for the same wavelength of incident radiation?
<img src="https://cdn-question-pool.getmarks.app/pyq/jee_main_2026_april_watermarked/04APRS1__q32_d1.png" />

**Options:**

- **(A)** $X_1$
- **(B)** $X_2$
- **(C)** $X_3$
- **(D)** All the metals will give out photo electrons with same kinetic energies.

<details>
<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Correct Option:** **(A) $X_1$**

**Detailed Solution:**

From Einstein's photoelectric equation, the maximum kinetic energy of emitted photoelectrons is given by:

$K_{\text{max}} = \dfrac{hc}{\lambda} - \Phi$

where $\lambda$ is the wavelength of incident radiation and $\Phi$ is the work function of the metal.

The work function is related to the threshold frequency $\nu_0$ by $\Phi = h\nu_0$. From the given graph, the x-intercept represents the threshold frequency. We can observe that:

$\nu_0(X_1) < \nu_0(X_2) < \nu_0(X_3)$

Therefore, the work functions of the metals follow the order:

$\Phi(X_1) < \Phi(X_2) < \Phi(X_3)$

For the same wavelength $\lambda$ of incident radiation, the term $\dfrac{hc}{\lambda}$ is constant. Thus, the kinetic energy $K_{\text{max}}$ will be greatest for the metal with the smallest work function.

Since metal $X_1$ has the minimum work function, it will emit electrons with the greatest kinetic energy.

Answer: $X_1$

</details>

### Q6. JEE Main 2026 (02 April Shift 1)

*Type:* `singleCorrect` | *Level:* `2`

For a certain metal, when monochromatic light of wavelength $\lambda$ is incident, the stopping potential for photoelectrons is $3V_0$. When the same metal is illuminated by light of wavelength $2\lambda$, then the stopping potential becomes $V_0$. The threshold wavelength for photoelectric emission for the given metal is $\alpha\lambda$. The value of $\alpha$ is _______.

**Options:**

- **(A)** $1$
- **(B)** $4$
- **(C)** $2$
- **(D)** $3$

<details>
<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Correct Option:** **(B) $4$**

**Detailed Solution:**

Using Einstein's photoelectric equation:

$eV_s = \dfrac{hc}{\lambda} - \Phi$

For the first case:

$3eV_0 = \dfrac{hc}{\lambda} - \Phi$

For the second case:

$eV_0 = \dfrac{hc}{2\lambda} - \Phi$

Multiplying the second equation by $3$ and equating with the first equation:

$\dfrac{hc}{\lambda} - \Phi = \dfrac{3hc}{2\lambda} - 3\Phi$

$2\Phi = \dfrac{3hc}{2\lambda} - \dfrac{hc}{\lambda}$

$2\Phi = \dfrac{hc}{2\lambda}$

$\Phi = \dfrac{hc}{4\lambda}$

Since the work function $\Phi = \dfrac{hc}{\lambda_0}$, where $\lambda_0$ is the threshold wavelength:

$\dfrac{hc}{\lambda_0} = \dfrac{hc}{4\lambda}$

$\lambda_0 = 4\lambda$

Given $\lambda_0 = \alpha\lambda$, we get $\alpha = 4$.

Answer: $4$

</details>

### Q7. JEE Main 2026 (28 January Shift 2)

*Type:* `singleCorrect` | *Level:* `2`

Number of photons of equal energy emitted per second by a 6 mW laser source operating at 663 nm is $\_\_\_\_$. 
 (Given : $\mathrm{h}=6.63 \times 10^{-34} \mathrm{~J}. \mathrm{s}$ and $\mathrm{c}=3 \times 10^{8} \mathrm{~m} / \mathrm{s}$)

**Options:**

- **(A)** $2 \times 10^{16}$
- **(B)** $10 \times 10^{15}$
- **(C)** $5 \times 10^{15}$
- **(D)** $5 \times 10^{16}$

<details>
<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Correct Option:** **(A) $2 \times 10^{16}$**

**Detailed Solution:**

Power of laser: $P = 6$ mW $= 6 \times 10^{-3}$ J/s
Energy of a single photon at wavelength $\lambda = 663$ nm:
$E = h\nu = \frac{hc}{\lambda} = \frac{6.63 \times 10^{-34} \times 3 \times 10^8}{663 \times 10^{-9}}$
$E = \frac{6.63 \times 3}{663} \times 10^{-34+8+9} = \frac{19.89}{663} \times 10^{-17} = 3 \times 10^{-19}$ J
Number of photons emitted per second:
$N = \frac{\text{Total Energy per second}}{\text{Energy per photon}} = \frac{6 \times 10^{-3}}{3 \times 10^{-19}} = 2 \times 10^{16}$

</details>

---

### Q8. JEE Main 2026 (24 January Shift 2)

*Type:* `singleCorrect` | *Level:* `2`

When a light of a given wavelength falls on a metallic surface the stopping potential for photoelectrons is 3.2 V. If a second light having wavelength twice of first light is used, the stopping potential drops to 0.7 V. The wavelength of first light is $\_\_\_\_$ m. $\left(\mathrm{h}=6.63 \times 10^{-34} \mathrm{~J}. \mathrm{s}, \mathrm{e}=1.6 \times 10^{-19} \mathrm{C}, \mathrm{c}=3 \times 10^{8} \mathrm{~m} / \mathrm{s}\right)$

**Options:**

- **(A)** $2.5 \times 10^{-7}$
- **(B)** $2.2 \times 10^{-8}$
- **(C)** $3.1 \times 10^{-7}$
- **(D)** $2.9 \times 10^{-8}$

<details>
<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Correct Option:** **(A) $2.5 \times 10^{-7}$**

**Detailed Solution:**

Using Einstein's photoelectric equation with stopping potentials:
For first light, $h\nu_1 = \phi + eV_{s1}$.
For second light with $\lambda_2 = 2\lambda_1$ (so $\nu_2 = \nu_1/2$): $h\nu_1/2 = \phi + eV_{s2}$.
From given values: $h\nu_1 - 3.2e = h\nu_1/2 - 0.7e$ gives $h\nu_1/2 = 2.5e$.
Thus $\lambda_1 = \frac{hc}{5e} = \frac{6.63 \times 10^{-34} \times 3 \times 10^8}{5 \times 1.6 \times 10^{-19}} \approx 2.5 \times 10^{-7}$ m.

</details>

### Q9. JEE Main 2026 (22 January Shift 2)

*Type:* `singleCorrect` | *Level:* `2`

Light is incident on a metallic plate having work function $110 \times 10^{-20} \mathrm{~J}$. If the produced photoelectrons have zero kinetic energy then the angular frequency of the incident light is $\_\_\_\_$ rad/s. $\left(\mathrm{h}=6.63 \times 10^{-34} \mathrm{~J}. \mathrm{s}\right)$.

**Options:**

- **(A)** $1.04 \times 10^{13}$
- **(B)** $1.04 \times 10^{16}$
- **(C)** $1.66 \times 10^{16}$
- **(D)** $1.66 \times 10^{15}$

<details>
<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Correct Option:** **(B) $1.04 \times 10^{16}$**

**Detailed Solution:**

From Einstein's photoelectric equation, $h\nu = W + KE$.
With zero kinetic energy, $h\nu = W$.
The frequency is $\nu = \frac{W}{h} = \frac{110 \times 10^{-20}}{6.63 \times 10^{-34}} = 16.59 \times 10^{14}$ Hz.
The angular frequency is $\omega = 2\pi\nu = 2\pi \times 16.59 \times 10^{14}$
$= 104.25 \times 10^{14} = 1.04 \times 10^{16}$ rad/s.

</details>

---

### Q10. JEE Main 2026 (21 January Shift 1)

*Type:* `singleCorrect` | *Level:* `2`

A light wave described by $E=60\left[\sin \left(3 \times 10^{15}\right) t+\sin \left(12 \times 10^{15}\right) t\right]$ (in SI units) falls on a metal surface of work function 2.8 eV. The maximum kinetic energy of ejected photoelectron is (approximately) $\_\_\_\_$ eV. $\left(h=6.6 \times 10^{-34} \mathrm{~J}\right.$. s. and $\left.e=1.6 \times 10^{-19} \mathrm{C}\right)$

**Options:**

- **(A)** 7.8
- **(B)** 6.0
- **(C)** 5.1
- **(D)** 3.8

<details>
<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Correct Option:** **(C) 5.1**

**Detailed Solution:**

Light contains two frequencies: $\omega_1 = 3 \times 10^{15}$ rad/s and $\omega_2 = 12 \times 10^{15}$ rad/s
For maximum KE, use higher frequency $\omega_2$.
Energy: $E_2 = \frac{h\omega_2}{2\pi} = \frac{6.6 \times 10^{-34} \times 12 \times 10^{15}}{2\pi}$
$= \frac{79.2 \times 10^{-19}}{6.28} = 12.6 \times 10^{-19}$ J $= 7.9$ eV
$KE_{max} = E_2 - \phi = 7.9 - 2.8 = 5.1$ eV

</details>

### Q11. JEE Main 2025 (4 Apr Shift 1)

*Type:* `singleCorrect` | *Level:* `3`

A small mirror of mass $m$ is suspended by a massless thread of length $l$. Then the small angle through which the thread will be deflected when a short pulse of laser of energy $E$ falls normal on the mirror ( $\mathrm{c}=$ speed of light in vacuum and $\mathrm{g}=$ acceleration due to gravity)

**Options:**

- **(A)** $\theta=\frac{3 \mathrm{E}}{4 \mathrm{mc} \sqrt{\mathrm{g} l}}$
- **(B)** $\theta=\frac{\mathrm{E}}{\mathrm{mc} \sqrt{\mathrm{g} l}}$
- **(C)** $\theta=\frac{\mathrm{E}}{2 \mathrm{mc} \sqrt{\mathrm{gl}}}$
- **(D)** $\theta=\frac{2 \mathrm{E}}{\mathrm{mc} \sqrt{\mathrm{g} l}}$

<details>
<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Correct Option:** **(D) $\theta=\frac{2 \mathrm{E}}{\mathrm{mc} \sqrt{\mathrm{g} l}}$**

**Detailed Solution:**

<img src="https://cdn-question-pool.getmarks.app/pyq/jee_main/fW0uZcgK018vDZEYwSvHZ46KMrQ2IhUobex-pkgQmyg.original.fullsize.png"/>
Force due to beam assuming complete reflection $\mathrm{F}=\frac{2 \mathrm{P}}{\mathrm{C}}=\frac{2}{\mathrm{C}} \frac{\mathrm{dE}}{\mathrm{dt}} ; \mathrm{P}$ is power
So change in momentum of mirror.
$\mathrm{m}(\mathrm{~V}-0)=\int \mathrm{Fdt}=\frac{2}{\mathrm{C}} \int \mathrm{dE}=\frac{2 \mathrm{E}}{\mathrm{C}}$
Now using work energy theorem ...(1)<img src="https://cdn-question-pool.getmarks.app/pyq/jee_main/BUExWy7_Bp9OsYRjsXmfw6qpD0qArobboYep9jU9v2s.original.fullsize.png"/>
$\begin{aligned}
& \mathrm{W}_{\mathrm{g}}=\Delta \mathrm{k} \\
& -\mathrm{mg} \ell(1-\cos \theta)=0-\frac{1}{2} \mathrm{mv}^2 \\
& \mathrm{~g} \ell\left(2 \sin ^2 \frac{\theta}{2}\right)=\frac{\mathrm{v}^2}{2}
\end{aligned}$
as $\theta$ is small
$\begin{aligned} & \mathrm{g} \ell 2\left(\frac{\theta}{2}\right)^2=\frac{1}{2} \frac{4 \mathrm{E}^2}{\mathrm{~m}^2 \mathrm{c}^2} \\ & \mathrm{~g} \ell \theta^2=\frac{4 \mathrm{E}^2}{\mathrm{~m}^2 \mathrm{c}^2} \\ & \theta=\frac{2 \mathrm{E}}{\mathrm{mc} \sqrt{\mathrm{g} \ell}}\end{aligned}$

</details>

Given below are two statements: one is labelled as Assertion A and the other is labelled as Reason $\mathrm{R}$

Reason R : Increase in intensity of light increases the rate of photoelectrons emitted, provided the frequency of incident light is greater than threshold frequency.

### Q13. JEE Main 2025 (3 Apr Shift 1)

*Type:* `singleCorrect` | *Level:* `1`

The work function of a metal is 3 eV. The color of the visible light that is required to cause emission of photoelectrons is

**Options:**

- **(A)** Green
- **(B)** Blue
- **(C)** Red
- **(D)** Yellow

<details>
<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Correct Option:** **(B) Blue**

**Detailed Solution:**

$\begin{aligned}
& (\mathrm{KE})_{\max }=\frac{\mathrm{hc}}{\lambda}-\phi \\
& \frac{\mathrm{hc}}{\lambda} \gt \phi[\text { for emission }] \\
& \lambda \lt \frac{\mathrm{hc}}{\phi} \Rightarrow \lambda \lt \frac{1242}{3} \mathrm{~nm}
\end{aligned}$
So blue light option (B)

</details>

### Q14. JEE Main 2025 (3 Apr Shift 1)

*Type:* `singleCorrect` | *Level:* `2`

The radiation pressure exerted by a 450 W light source on a perfectly reflecting surface placed at 2 m away from it, is :

**Options:**

- **(A)** $1.5 \times 10^{-8}$ Pascals
- **(B)** 0
- **(C)** $6 \times 10^{-8}$ Pascals
- **(D)** $3 \times 10^{-8}$ Pascals

<details>
<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Correct Option:** **(C) $6 \times 10^{-8}$ Pascals**

**Detailed Solution:**

$P_{\mathrm{rad}}=\frac{2 I}{\mathrm{C}}$
Where $\mathrm{I}=$ intensity at surface
$\mathrm{C}=$ Speed of light
$\begin{aligned}
& I=\frac{\text { Power }}{\text { Area }}=\frac{450}{4 \pi \mathrm{r}^2} \\
& =\frac{450}{4 \pi \times 4}=\frac{450}{16 \pi} \\
& P_{\mathrm{rad}}=\frac{2 \times 450}{16 \pi \times 3 \times 10^8}=\frac{150}{8 \pi \times 10^8}
\end{aligned}$
$=5.97 \times 10^{-8} \approx 6 \times 10^{-8}$ Pascals

</details>

### Q15. JEE Main 2025 (2 Apr Shift 1)

*Type:* `singleCorrect` | *Level:* `2`

A monochromatic light is incident on a metallic plate having work function $\phi$. An electron, emitted normally to the plate from a point $A$ with maximum kinetic energy, enters a constant magnetic field, perpendicular to the initial velocity of electron. The electron passes through a curve and hits back the plate at a point $B$. The distance between A and B is :
(Given : The magnitude of charge of an electron is e and mass is $\mathrm{m}, \mathrm{h}$ is Planck's constant and c is velocity of light. Take the magnetic field exists throughout the path of electron)

**Options:**

- **(A)** $\sqrt{2 \mathrm{~m}\left(\frac{\mathrm{hc}}{\lambda}-\phi\right)} / \mathrm{eB}$
- **(B)** $\sqrt{\mathrm{m}\left(\frac{\mathrm{hc}}{\lambda}-\phi\right)} / \mathrm{eB}$
- **(C)** $\sqrt{8 \mathrm{~m}\left(\frac{\mathrm{hc}}{\lambda}-\phi\right)} / \mathrm{eB}$
- **(D)** $2 \sqrt{\mathrm{~m}\left(\frac{\mathrm{hc}}{\lambda}-\phi\right)} / \mathrm{eB}$

<details>
<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Correct Option:** **(C) $\sqrt{8 \mathrm{~m}\left(\frac{\mathrm{hc}}{\lambda}-\phi\right)} / \mathrm{eB}$**

**Detailed Solution:**

$\begin{aligned} & \mathrm{KE}_{\max }=\frac{\mathrm{hc}}{\lambda}-\phi \\ & \mathrm{p}=\sqrt{2 \mathrm{mK}_{\max }} \\ & \mathrm{p}=\sqrt{2 \mathrm{~m}\left(\frac{\mathrm{hc}}{\lambda}-\phi\right)} \\ & \mathrm{d}_{\mathrm{A}-\mathrm{B}}=2 \mathrm{R} \\ & =2\left[\frac{\mathrm{p}}{\mathrm{qB}}\right]\end{aligned}$
$\mathrm{d}_{\mathrm{AB}}=\frac{2 \sqrt{2 \mathrm{~m}\left(\frac{\mathrm{hc}}{\lambda}-\phi\right)}}{\mathrm{eB}}=\frac{\sqrt{8 \mathrm{~m}\left(\frac{\mathrm{hc}}{\lambda}-\phi\right)}}{\mathrm{eB}}$

</details>

### Q17. JEE Main 2025 (24 Jan Shift 2)

*Type:* `singleCorrect` | *Level:* `1`

In photoelectric effect, the stopping potential $\left(\mathrm{V}_0\right) \mathrm{v} / \mathrm{s}$ frequency $(\nu)$ curve is plotted.
( h is the Planck's constant and $\phi_0$ is work function of metal )
(A) $\mathrm{V}_0 \mathrm{v} / \mathrm{s} \nu$ is linear.
(B) The slope of $\mathrm{V}_0 \mathrm{v} / \mathrm{s} \nu$ curve $=\frac{\phi_0}{\mathrm{~h}}$
(C) h constant is related to the slope of $\mathrm{V}_0 \mathrm{v} / \mathrm{s} \nu$ line.
(D) The value of electric charge of electron is not required to determine $h$ using the $V_0 \mathrm{v} / \mathrm{s} \nu$ curve.
(E) The work function can be estimated without knowing the value of $h$.

Choose the correct answer from the options given below :

**Options:**

- **(A)** (C) and (D) only
- **(B)** (A), (C) and (E) only
- **(C)** (A), (B) and (C) only
- **(D)** (D) and (E) only

<details>
<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Correct Option:** **(B) (A), (C) and (E) only**

**Detailed Solution:**

$\begin{aligned}
& \mathrm{hv}=\phi+\mathrm{KE}_{\max } \\
& \mathrm{KE}_{\max }=\mathrm{eV}_0 \\
& \mathrm{~V}_0=\frac{\mathrm{hv}-\phi}{\mathrm{e}}
\end{aligned}$

(B) Slope
$\mathrm{v}_0=\left(\frac{\mathrm{h}}{\mathrm{e}}\right) \mathrm{v}-\frac{\phi}{\mathrm{e}} \text { Wrong }$

Slope $\frac{h}{e}$
(C) Correct
(D) Incorrect
(E) Correct

</details>

### Q18. JEE Main 2025 (23 Jan Shift 2)

*Type:* `singleCorrect` | *Level:* `1`

In photoelectric effect an em-wave is incident on a metal surface and electrons are ejected from the surface. If the work function of the metal is 2.14 eV and stopping potential is 2 V , what is the wavelength of the em-wave ?
(Given hc $=1242 \mathrm{eVnm}$ where h is the Planck's constant and c is the speed of light in vaccum.)

**Options:**

- **(A)** 300 nm
- **(B)** 400 nm
- **(C)** 600 nm
- **(D)** 200 nm

<details>
<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Correct Option:** **(A) 300 nm**

**Detailed Solution:**

$\begin{aligned}
& \phi=2.14 \\
& V_S=2 \mathrm{~V}
\end{aligned}$
Using photoelectric equation.
$\begin{aligned}
& \frac{h c}{\lambda}=2.14+2=4.14 \mathrm{eV} \\
& \lambda=\frac{1242}{4.14}=300 \mathrm{~nm}
\end{aligned}$

</details>

---

### Q19. JEE Main 2025 (22 Jan Shift 2)

*Type:* `singleCorrect` | *Level:* `1`

A light source of wavelength $\lambda$ illuminates a metal surface and electrons are ejected with maximum kinetic energy of 2 eV . If the same surface is illuminated by a light source of wavelength $\frac{\lambda}{2}$, then the maximum kinetic energy of ejected electrons will be (The work function of metal is 1 eV )

**Options:**

- **(A)** 3 eV
- **(B)** 2 eV
- **(C)** 6 eV
- **(D)** 5 eV

<details>
<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Correct Option:** **(D) 5 eV**

**Detailed Solution:**

Einstein's photoelectric equation
$\begin{aligned}
& \mathrm{KE}=\frac{h c}{\lambda}=\phi_0 \\
& 2 \mathrm{eV}=\frac{h c}{\lambda}-1 \mathrm{eV} \\
& \frac{h c}{\lambda}=3 \mathrm{eV} \\
& \mathrm{KE}^{\prime}=\frac{h c}{(\lambda / 2)}-\phi_0=6 \mathrm{eV}-1 \mathrm{eV}
\end{aligned}$
$=5 \mathrm{eV}$

</details>

---

### Q20. JEE Main 2025 (22 Jan Shift 1)

*Type:* `singleCorrect` | *Level:* `2`

The work functions of cesium (Cs) and lithium (Li) metals are 1.9 eV and 2.5 eV , respectively. If we incident a light of wavelength 550 nm on these two metal surfaces, then photo-electric effect is possible for the case of

**Options:**

- **(A)** Both Cs and Li
- **(B)** Neither Cs nor Li
- **(C)** Cs only
- **(D)** Li only

<details>
<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Correct Option:** **(C) Cs only**

**Detailed Solution:**

\(E=\frac{1240 \mathrm{eV} \cdot \mathrm{~nm}}{550 \mathrm{~nm}}=2.25 \mathrm{eV}\)
Possible for \(C_s\) as \(E > \phi_{c s}\)
Not for Li as \(E < \phi_{L i}\)

</details>

### Q21. JEE Main 2024 (09 Apr Shift 2)

*Type:* `singleCorrect` | *Level:* `1`

UV light of $4.13 \mathrm{eV}$ is incident on a photosensitive metal surface having work function $3.13 \mathrm{eV}$. The maximum kinetic energy of ejected photoelectrons will be:

**Options:**

- **(A)** $4.13 \mathrm{eV}$
- **(B)** $3.13 \mathrm{eV}$
- **(C)** $1 \mathrm{eV}$
- **(D)** $7.26 \mathrm{eV}$

<details>
<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Correct Option:** **(C) $1 \mathrm{eV}$**

**Detailed Solution:**

$\begin{aligned} & \mathrm{E}_{\text {photon }}=(\text { work function })+\mathrm{K} \cdot \mathrm{E}_{\max } \\ & \therefore 4.13=3.13+\mathrm{K} \cdot \mathrm{E}_{\max } \\ & \therefore \mathrm{K}_{\mathrm{max}}=1 \mathrm{eV}\end{aligned}$

</details>

---

### Q22. JEE Main 2024 (08 Apr Shift 1)

*Type:* `singleCorrect` | *Level:* `2`

Average force exerted on a non-reflecting surface at normal incidence is $2.4 \times 10^{-4} \mathrm{~N}$. If $360 \mathrm{~W} / \mathrm{cm}^2$ is the light energy flux during span of 1 hour 30 minutes, Then the area of the surface is:

**Options:**

- **(A)** $0.2 \mathrm{~m}^2$
- **(B)** $20 \mathrm{~m}^2$
- **(C)** $0.1 \mathrm{~m}^2$
- **(D)** $0.02 \mathrm{~m}^2$

<details>
<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Correct Option:** **(D) $0.02 \mathrm{~m}^2$**

**Detailed Solution:**

$\begin{aligned} & \text { Pressure }=\frac{I}{C}=\frac{F}{A} \\ & \Rightarrow \frac{360}{10^{-4} \times 3 \times 10^8}=\frac{2.4 \times 10^{-4}}{A} \\ & \Rightarrow \mathrm{A}=2 \times 10^{-2} \mathrm{~m}^2=0.02 \mathrm{~m}^2\end{aligned}$

</details>

---

### Q23. JEE Main 2024 (06 Apr Shift 2)

*Type:* `singleCorrect` | *Level:* `2`

When UV light of wavelength $300 \mathrm{~nm}$ is incident on the metal surface having work function $2.13 \mathrm{eV}$, electron emission takes place. The stopping potential is:
(Given hc $=1240 \mathrm{eV} \mathrm{nm}$ )

**Options:**

- **(A)** $1.5 \mathrm{~V}$
- **(B)** $4.1 \mathrm{~V}$
- **(C)** $2 \mathrm{~V}$
- **(D)** $4 \mathrm{~V}$

<details>
<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Correct Option:** **(C) $2 \mathrm{~V}$**

**Detailed Solution:**

$\begin{aligned} & \frac{\mathrm{hc}}{\lambda}-\phi=\mathrm{e} . \mathrm{V}_{\mathrm{s}} \\ & \Rightarrow \frac{1240}{300} \mathrm{eV}-2.13 \mathrm{eV}=\mathrm{eVs} \\ & \Rightarrow 4.13 \mathrm{eV}-2.13 \mathrm{eV}=\mathrm{eVs} . \\ & \Rightarrow \text { So, } \quad \mathrm{V}_{\mathrm{s}}=2 \mathrm{volt}\end{aligned}$

</details>

### Q25. JEE Main 2024 (06 Apr Shift 1)

*Type:* `singleCorrect` | *Level:* `1`

In photoelectric experiment energy of $2.48 \mathrm{eV}$ irradiates a photo sensitive material. The stopping potential was measured to be $0.5 \mathrm{~V}$. Work function of the photo sensitive material is :

**Options:**

- **(A)** $1.68 \mathrm{eV}$
- **(B)** $2.48 \mathrm{eV}$
- **(C)** $1.98 \mathrm{eV}$
- **(D)** $0.5 \mathrm{eV}$

<details>
<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Correct Option:** **(C) $1.98 \mathrm{eV}$**

**Detailed Solution:**

$\begin{aligned} & \mathrm{eV} \mathrm{V}_{\mathrm{s}}=\mathrm{h} \nu-\phi \\ & 0.5 \mathrm{~V}=2.48-\phi \\ & \text { work function }(\phi)=2.48 \mathrm{~V}-0.5 \mathrm{~V}=1.98 \mathrm{~V}\end{aligned}$

</details>

### Q26. JEE Main 2024 (05 Apr Shift 2)

*Type:* `singleCorrect` | *Level:* `2`

Which of the following statement is not true about stopping potential $\left(\mathrm{V}_0\right)$ ?

- **(B)** It increases with increase in intensity of the incident light.

- **(D)** It depends upon frequency of the incident light.

<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Detailed Solution:**

### Q27. JEE Main 2024 (05 Apr Shift 1)

*Type:* `singleCorrect` | *Level:* `1`

Given below are two statements :
<img src="https://cdn-question-pool.getmarks.app/pyq/jee_main/Vv8V7jULCpikDwS2f-JwFuUXGOGm5PoxVNNvDZEQf_s.original.fullsize.png"/>
Statement I : Figure shows the variation of stopping potential with frequency $(v)$ for the two photosensitive materials $\mathrm{M}_1$ and $\mathrm{M}_2$. The slope gives value of $\frac{\mathrm{h}}{\mathrm{e}}$, where $\mathrm{h}$ is Planck's constant, $\mathrm{e}$ is the charge of electron.
Statement II : $\mathrm{M}_2$ will emit photoelectrons of greater kinetic energy for the incident radiation having same frequency.
In the light of the above statements, choose the most appropriate answer from the options given below.

**Options:**

- **(A)** Both Statement I and Statement II are correct
- **(B)** Statement I is incorrect but Statement II is correct
- **(C)** Both Statement I and Statement II are incorrect
- **(D)** Statement I is correct and Statement II is incorrect

<details>
<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Correct Option:** **(D) Statement I is correct and Statement II is incorrect**

**Detailed Solution:**

$\begin{aligned}
& \mathrm{eV}_0=\mathrm{hv}-\phi \\
& \mathrm{V}_0=\frac{\mathrm{h}}{\mathrm{e}} \mathrm{v}-\frac{\phi}{\mathrm{e}}
\end{aligned}$
$\mathrm{M}_2$ material has higher work function, so statement-(II) is incorrect.

</details>

### Q28. JEE Main 2024 (04 Apr Shift 2)

*Type:* `singleCorrect` | *Level:* `2`

Given below are two statements: one is labelled as Assertion $\mathbf{A}$ and the other is labelled as Reason R.
Assertion A: Number of photons increases with increase in frequency of light.
Reason R: Maximum kinetic energy of emitted electrons increases with the frequency of incident radiation.
In the light of the above statements, choose the most appropriate answer from the options given below:

**Options:**

- **(A)** Both $\mathbf{A}$ and $\mathbf{R}$ are correct and $\mathbf{R}$ is the correct explanation of $\mathbf{A}$.
- **(B)** Both $\mathbf{A}$ and $\mathbf{R}$ are correct and $\mathbf{R}$ is NOT the correct explanation of $\mathbf{A}$.
- **(C)** $\mathbf{A}$ is not correct but $\mathbf{R}$ is correct.
- **(D)** $\mathbf{A}$ is correct but $\mathbf{R}$ is not correct.

<details>
<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Correct Option:** **(C) $\mathbf{A}$ is not correct but $\mathbf{R}$ is correct.**

**Detailed Solution:**

Intensity of light $\mathrm{I}=\frac{\mathrm{nh} \nu}{\mathrm{A}}$
Here $\mathrm{n}$ is no. of photons per unit time.
$\mathrm{n}=\frac{\mathrm{IA}}{\mathrm{h} \nu}$ so on increasing frequency $v, \mathrm{n}$ decreases taking intensity constant.
$\mathrm{k}_{\max }=\mathrm{h} v-\phi$
So on increasing $v$, kinetic energy increases.

</details>

- **(B)** <img src="https://cdn-question-pool.getmarks.app/pyq/jee_main/A_NbeXasQ8BVh8T0yF65zXNQLLWuyWUy13Sml3PKFY4.original.fullsize.png">
- **(C)** <img src="https://cdn-question-pool.getmarks.app/pyq/jee_main/45AtURJyYVEJAnFe2apFDDpw3vwVhDYvBwM3jeyFDgU.original.fullsize.png">

### Q30. JEE Main 2024 (01 Feb Shift 2)

*Type:* `singleCorrect` | *Level:* `2`

<p>Monochromatic light of frequency <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mn>6</mn><mo>×</mo><msup><mn>10</mn><mn>14</mn></msup><mo> </mo><mi>Hz</mi></math> is produced by a laser. The power emitted is <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mn>2</mn><mo>×</mo><msup><mn>10</mn><mrow><mo>-</mo><mn>3</mn></mrow></msup><mo> </mo><mi mathvariant="normal">W</mi><mo>.</mo></math> How many photons per second on an average, are emitted by the source?</p><p>(Given <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mi mathvariant="normal">h</mi><mo>=</mo><mn>6</mn><mo>.</mo><mn>63</mn><mo>×</mo><msup><mn>10</mn><mrow><mo>-</mo><mn>34</mn></mrow></msup><mo> </mo><mi mathvariant="normal">J</mi><mo> </mo><mi mathvariant="normal">s</mi></math>)</p>

**Options:**

- **(A)** <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mn>9</mn><mo>×</mo><msup><mn>10</mn><mn>18</mn></msup></math>
- **(B)** <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mn>6</mn><mo>×</mo><msup><mn>10</mn><mn>15</mn></msup></math>
- **(C)** <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mn>5</mn><mo>×</mo><msup><mn>10</mn><mn>15</mn></msup></math>
- **(D)** <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mn>7</mn><mo>×</mo><msup><mn>10</mn><mn>16</mn></msup></math>

<details>
<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Correct Option:** **(C) <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mn>5</mn><mo>×</mo><msup><mn>10</mn><mn>15</mn></msup></math>**

**Detailed Solution:**

<p>Energy of a single photon is given by, <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mi>h</mi><mi>ν</mi></math>. Therefore, power can be written as </p><p><math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mi>P</mi><mo>=</mo><mi>n</mi><mi>h</mi><mi>v</mi></math></p><p><math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mo>⇒</mo><mi>n</mi><mo>=</mo><mfrac><mi>P</mi><mrow><mi>h</mi><mi>ν</mi></mrow></mfrac><mo>=</mo><mfrac><mrow><mn>2</mn><mo>×</mo><msup><mn>10</mn><mrow><mo>-</mo><mn>3</mn></mrow></msup></mrow><mrow><mn>6</mn><mo>.</mo><mn>63</mn><mo>×</mo><msup><mn>10</mn><mrow><mo>-</mo><mn>34</mn></mrow></msup><mo>×</mo><mn>6</mn><mo>×</mo><msup><mn>10</mn><mn>14</mn></msup></mrow></mfrac></math></p><p><math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mo>=</mo><mn>5</mn><mo>×</mo><msup><mn>10</mn><mn>15</mn></msup></math></p>

</details>

### Q31. JEE Main 2024 (31 Jan Shift 2)

*Type:* `singleCorrect` | *Level:* `1`

In a photoelectric effect experiment a light of frequency <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mn>1</mn><mo>.</mo><mn>5</mn></math> times the threshold frequency is made to fall on the surface of photosensitive material. Now if the frequency is halved and intensity is doubled, the number of photo electrons emitted will be:

**Options:**

- **(A)** Doubled
- **(B)** Quadrupled
- **(C)** Zero
- **(D)** Halved

<details>
<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Correct Option:** **(C) Zero**

**Detailed Solution:**

<p>Given, <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><msub><mi>f</mi><mi>i</mi></msub><mo>=</mo><msub><mi>f</mi><mn>0</mn></msub></math>. Now, <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mi>f</mi><mo>=</mo><mfrac><msub><mi>f</mi><mi>i</mi></msub><mn>2</mn></mfrac><mo>=</mo><mfrac><msub><mi>f</mi><mn>0</mn></msub><mn>2</mn></mfrac></math>.</p><p>Since <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mfrac><msub><mi>f</mi><mn>0</mn></msub><mn>2</mn></mfrac><mo>&lt;</mo><msub><mi>f</mi><mn>0</mn></msub></math> i.e. the incident frequency is less than threshold frequency. Hence, there will be no emission of photoelectrons.</p><p>Therefore, current <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mo>=</mo><mn>0</mn></math> and no photo electrons will be emitted.</p>

</details>

### Q32. JEE Main 2024 (31 Jan Shift 1)

*Type:* `singleCorrect` | *Level:* `2`

When a metal surface is illuminated by light of wavelength <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mi>λ</mi></math>, the stopping potential is <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mn>8</mn><mo> </mo><mi mathvariant="normal">V</mi></math>. When the same surface is illuminated by light of wavelength <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mn>3</mn><mi>λ</mi></math>, stopping potential is <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mn>2</mn><mo> </mo><mi mathvariant="normal">V</mi></math>. The threshold wavelength for this surface is :

**Options:**

- **(A)** <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mn>5</mn><mi>λ</mi></math>
- **(B)** <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mn>3</mn><mi>λ</mi></math>
- **(C)** <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mn>9</mn><mi>λ</mi></math>
- **(D)** <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mn>4</mn><mo>.</mo><mn>5</mn><mi>λ</mi></math>

<details>
<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Correct Option:** **(C) <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mn>9</mn><mi>λ</mi></math>**

**Detailed Solution:**

<p>From photoelectric effect equation, we can write <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mi>E</mi><mo>=</mo><mi>ϕ</mi><mo>+</mo><msub><mi>K</mi><mrow><mi>m</mi><mi>a</mi><mi>x</mi></mrow></msub></math></p><p>and work function can be written as <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mi>ϕ</mi><mo>=</mo><mfrac><mrow><mi>h</mi><mi>c</mi></mrow><msub><mi>λ</mi><mn>0</mn></msub></mfrac></math>.</p><p>Also, maximum kinetic energy <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><msub><mi>K</mi><mrow><mi>m</mi><mi>a</mi><mi>x</mi></mrow></msub><mo>=</mo><mi>e</mi><msub><mi>V</mi><mn>0</mn></msub></math></p><p>For first case:</p><p><math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mn>8</mn><mi>e</mi><mo>=</mo><mfrac><mrow><mi>h</mi><mi>c</mi></mrow><mi>λ</mi></mfrac><mo>-</mo><mfrac><mrow><mi>h</mi><mi>c</mi></mrow><msub><mi>λ</mi><mn>0</mn></msub></mfrac></math>   ... (i)</p><p>For second case:</p><p><math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mn>2</mn><mi>e</mi><mo>=</mo><mfrac><mrow><mi>h</mi><mi>c</mi></mrow><mrow><mn>3</mn><mi>λ</mi></mrow></mfrac><mo>-</mo><mfrac><mrow><mi>h</mi><mi>c</mi></mrow><msub><mi>λ</mi><mn>0</mn></msub></mfrac></math>    ... (ii)</p><p>After using equation (ii) in (i), we get</p><p><math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mn>4</mn><mfenced><mrow><mfrac><mrow><mi>h</mi><mi>c</mi></mrow><mrow><mn>3</mn><mi>λ</mi></mrow></mfrac><mo>-</mo><mfrac><mrow><mi>h</mi><mi>c</mi></mrow><msub><mi>λ</mi><mn>0</mn></msub></mfrac></mrow></mfenced><mo>=</mo><mfrac><mrow><mi>h</mi><mi>c</mi></mrow><mi>λ</mi></mfrac><mo>-</mo><mfrac><mrow><mi>h</mi><mi>c</mi></mrow><msub><mi>λ</mi><mn>0</mn></msub></mfrac></math></p><p><math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mo>⇒</mo><msub><mi>λ</mi><mn>0</mn></msub><mo>=</mo><mn>9</mn><mi>λ</mi></math></p>

</details>

### Q33. JEE Main 2024 (30 Jan Shift 2)

*Type:* `singleCorrect` | *Level:* `1`

<p>For the photoelectric effect, the maximum kinetic energy <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mfenced><msub><mi>E</mi><mi>k</mi></msub></mfenced></math> of the photoelectrons is plotted against the frequency <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mo>(</mo><mi>v</mi><mo>)</mo></math> of the incident photons as shown in figure. The slope of the graph gives</p><img src="https://cdn-question-pool.getmarks.app/pyq/jee_main/ed1907e6-cf04-40e1-86a4-24ac978e0eec.png" style="width: 250px; height: 181px;"/>

**Options:**

- **(A)** Ratio of Planck’s constant to electric charge
- **(B)** Work function of the metal
- **(C)** Charge of electron
- **(D)** Planck’s constant

<details>
<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Correct Option:** **(D) Planck’s constant**

**Detailed Solution:**

<blockquote><div>Comparing Einstein's equation <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><msub><mi>K</mi><mi>max</mi></msub><mo>=</mo><mi>h</mi><mi>v</mi><mo>-</mo><mi>h</mi><msub><mi>v</mi><mn>0</mn></msub><mo>,</mo></math> with <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mi>y</mi><mo>=</mo><mi>m</mi><mi>x</mi><mo>+</mo><mi>c</mi><mo>,</mo></math> we get slope, <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mi>m</mi><mo>=</mo><mi>h</mi></math>, which is Planck’s constant.</div></blockquote>

</details>

### Q34. JEE Main 2024 (30 Jan Shift 2)

*Type:* `singleCorrect` | *Level:* `2`

If the total energy transferred to a surface in time <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mi>t</mi></math> is <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mn>6</mn><mo>.</mo><mn>48</mn><mo>×</mo><msup><mn>10</mn><mn>5</mn></msup><mo> </mo><mi mathvariant="normal">J</mi></math>, then the magnitude of the total momentum delivered to this surface for complete absorption will be :

**Options:**

- **(A)** <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mn>2</mn><mo>.</mo><mn>46</mn><mo>×</mo><msup><mn>10</mn><mrow><mo>-</mo><mn>3</mn></mrow></msup><mo> </mo><mi>kg</mi><mo> </mo><mi mathvariant="normal">m</mi><mo> </mo><msup><mi mathvariant="normal">s</mi><mrow><mo>-</mo><mn>1</mn></mrow></msup></math>
- **(B)** <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mn>2</mn><mo>.</mo><mn>16</mn><mo>×</mo><msup><mn>10</mn><mrow><mo>-</mo><mn>3</mn></mrow></msup><mo> </mo><mi>kg</mi><mo> </mo><mi mathvariant="normal">m</mi><mo> </mo><msup><mi mathvariant="normal">s</mi><mrow><mo>-</mo><mn>1</mn></mrow></msup></math>
- **(C)** <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mn>1</mn><mo>.</mo><mn>58</mn><mo>×</mo><msup><mn>10</mn><mrow><mo>-</mo><mn>3</mn></mrow></msup><mo> </mo><mi>kg</mi><mo> </mo><mi mathvariant="normal">m</mi><mo> </mo><msup><mi mathvariant="normal">s</mi><mrow><mo>-</mo><mn>1</mn></mrow></msup></math>
- **(D)** <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mn>4</mn><mo>.</mo><mn>32</mn><mo>×</mo><msup><mn>10</mn><mrow><mo>-</mo><mn>3</mn></mrow></msup><mo> </mo><mi>kg</mi><mo> </mo><mi mathvariant="normal">m</mi><mo> </mo><msup><mi mathvariant="normal">s</mi><mrow><mo>-</mo><mn>1</mn></mrow></msup></math>

<details>
<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Correct Option:** **(B) <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mn>2</mn><mo>.</mo><mn>16</mn><mo>×</mo><msup><mn>10</mn><mrow><mo>-</mo><mn>3</mn></mrow></msup><mo> </mo><mi>kg</mi><mo> </mo><mi mathvariant="normal">m</mi><mo> </mo><msup><mi mathvariant="normal">s</mi><mrow><mo>-</mo><mn>1</mn></mrow></msup></math>**

**Detailed Solution:**

<blockquote><p>As we know,</p><p><math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mi>p</mi><mo>=</mo><mfrac><mi>E</mi><mi>c</mi></mfrac><mo>,</mo></math> where <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mi>c</mi></math> is velocity of light.</p><p>Therefore, <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mi>p</mi><mo>=</mo><mfrac><mrow><mn>6</mn><mo>.</mo><mn>48</mn><mo>×</mo><msup><mn>10</mn><mn>5</mn></msup></mrow><mrow><mn>3</mn><mo>×</mo><msup><mn>10</mn><mn>8</mn></msup></mrow></mfrac><mo>=</mo><mn>2</mn><mo>.</mo><mn>16</mn><mo>×</mo><msup><mn>10</mn><mrow><mo>-</mo><mn>3</mn></mrow></msup><mo> </mo><mi>kg</mi><mo> </mo><mi mathvariant="normal">m</mi><mo> </mo><msup><mi mathvariant="normal">s</mi><mrow><mo>-</mo><mn>1</mn></mrow></msup></math></p></blockquote>

</details>

### Q35. JEE Main 2024 (30 Jan Shift 1)

*Type:* `singleCorrect` | *Level:* `1`

The work function of a substance is <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mn>3</mn><mo>.</mo><mn>0</mn><mo> </mo><mi>eV</mi></math>. The longest wavelength of light that can cause the emission of photoelectrons from this substance is approximately:

**Options:**

- **(A)** <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mn>215</mn><mo> </mo><mi>nm</mi></math>
- **(B)** <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mn>414</mn><mo> </mo><mi>nm</mi></math>
- **(C)** <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mn>400</mn><mo> </mo><mi>nm</mi></math>
- **(D)** <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mn>200</mn><mo> </mo><mi>nm</mi></math>

<details>
<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Correct Option:** **(B) <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mn>414</mn><mo> </mo><mi>nm</mi></math>**

**Detailed Solution:**

<p>For longest wavelength, the emitted electron will have nearly zero kinetic energy.</p><p>Therefore,</p><p><math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mi>λ</mi><mo>=</mo><mfrac><mrow><mi>h</mi><mi>c</mi></mrow><msub><mi>W</mi><mi>e</mi></msub></mfrac></math></p><p><math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mo>⇒</mo><mi>λ</mi><mo>=</mo><mfrac><mrow><mn>1240</mn><mo> </mo><mi>nm</mi><mo>-</mo><mi>eV</mi></mrow><mrow><mn>3</mn><mo> </mo><mi>eV</mi></mrow></mfrac></math></p><p><math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mo>⇒</mo><mi>λ</mi><mo>=</mo><mn>413</mn><mo>.</mo><mn>33</mn><mo> </mo><mi>nm</mi></math></p><p><math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mo>⇒</mo><msub><mi>λ</mi><mi>max</mi></msub><mo>≈</mo><mn>414</mn><mo> </mo><mi>nm</mi></math></p>

</details>

---

### Q36. JEE Main 2024 (29 Jan Shift 2)

*Type:* `singleCorrect` | *Level:* `2`

Two sources of light emit with a power of <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mn>200</mn><mo> </mo><mi mathvariant="normal">W</mi></math>. The ratio of number of photons of visible light emitted by each source having wavelengths <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mn>300</mn><mo> </mo><mi>nm</mi></math> and <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mn>500</mn><mo> </mo><mi>nm</mi></math> respectively, will be :

**Options:**

- **(A)** <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mn>1</mn><mo>:</mo><mn>5</mn></math>
- **(B)** <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mn>1</mn><mo>:</mo><mn>3</mn></math>
- **(C)** <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mn>5</mn><mo>:</mo><mn>3</mn></math>
- **(D)** <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mn>3</mn><mo>:</mo><mn>5</mn></math>

<details>
<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Correct Option:** **(D) <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mn>3</mn><mo>:</mo><mn>5</mn></math>**

**Detailed Solution:**

<p>Let <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><msub><mi>n</mi><mn>1</mn></msub></math> and <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><msub><mi>n</mi><mn>2</mn></msub></math> be the number of photons coming out from the first and the second source of light respectively per second.</p><p>So, for the first source,</p><p><math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><msub><mi>n</mi><mn>1</mn></msub><mo>×</mo><mfrac><mrow><mi>h</mi><mi>c</mi></mrow><msub><mi>λ</mi><mn>1</mn></msub></mfrac><mo>=</mo><mn>200</mn><mo> </mo><mo> </mo><mo> </mo><mo>.</mo><mo>.</mo><mo>.</mo><mfenced><mn>1</mn></mfenced></math></p><p>And, for the second source,</p><p><math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><msub><mi>n</mi><mn>2</mn></msub><mo>×</mo><mfrac><mrow><mi>h</mi><mi>c</mi></mrow><msub><mi>λ</mi><mn>2</mn></msub></mfrac><mo>=</mo><mn>200</mn><mo> </mo><mo> </mo><mo> </mo><mo>.</mo><mo>.</mo><mo>.</mo><mfenced><mn>2</mn></mfenced></math></p><p>From equations (1) and (2), it implies that</p><p><math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mfrac><msub><mi>n</mi><mn>1</mn></msub><msub><mi>n</mi><mn>2</mn></msub></mfrac><mo>=</mo><mfrac><msub><mi>λ</mi><mn>1</mn></msub><msub><mi>λ</mi><mn>2</mn></msub></mfrac><mspace linebreak="newline"></mspace><mo>=</mo><mfrac><mn>300</mn><mn>500</mn></mfrac><mspace linebreak="newline"></mspace><mo>⇒</mo><mfrac><msub><mi>n</mi><mn>1</mn></msub><msub><mi>n</mi><mn>2</mn></msub></mfrac><mo>=</mo><mfrac><mn>3</mn><mn>5</mn></mfrac></math></p>

</details>

---

### Q37. JEE Main 2024 (27 Jan Shift 2)

*Type:* `singleCorrect` | *Level:* `1`

The threshold frequency of a metal with work function <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mn>6</mn><mo>.</mo><mn>63</mn><mo> </mo><mi>eV</mi></math> is :

**Options:**

- **(A)** <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mn>16</mn><mo>×</mo><msup><mn>10</mn><mn>15</mn></msup><mo> </mo><mi>Hz</mi></math>
- **(B)** <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mn>16</mn><mo>×</mo><msup><mn>10</mn><mn>12</mn></msup><mo> </mo><mi>Hz</mi></math>
- **(C)** <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mn>1</mn><mo>.</mo><mn>6</mn><mo>×</mo><msup><mn>10</mn><mn>12</mn></msup><mo> </mo><mi>Hz</mi></math>
- **(D)** <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mn>1</mn><mo>.</mo><mn>6</mn><mo>×</mo><msup><mn>10</mn><mn>15</mn></msup><mo> </mo><mi>Hz</mi></math>

<details>
<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Correct Option:** **(D) <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mn>1</mn><mo>.</mo><mn>6</mn><mo>×</mo><msup><mn>10</mn><mn>15</mn></msup><mo> </mo><mi>Hz</mi></math>**

**Detailed Solution:**

<p>The formula to calculate the work function of the metal is given by</p><p><math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><msub><mi>ϕ</mi><mn>0</mn></msub><mo>=</mo><mi>h</mi><msub><mi>v</mi><mn>0</mn></msub><mo> </mo><mo> </mo><mo> </mo><mo>.</mo><mo>.</mo><mo>.</mo><mfenced><mn>1</mn></mfenced></math></p><p>From equation (1), it follows that</p><p><math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mn>6</mn><mo>.</mo><mn>63</mn><mo>×</mo><mn>1</mn><mo>.</mo><mn>6</mn><mo>×</mo><msup><mn>10</mn><mrow><mo>-</mo><mn>19</mn></mrow></msup><mo>=</mo><mn>6</mn><mo>.</mo><mn>63</mn><mo>×</mo><msup><mn>10</mn><mrow><mo>-</mo><mn>34</mn></mrow></msup><msub><mi>v</mi><mn>0</mn></msub><mspace linebreak="newline"></mspace><mo>⇒</mo><msub><mi>v</mi><mn>0</mn></msub><mo>=</mo><mfrac><mrow><mn>1</mn><mo>.</mo><mn>6</mn><mo>×</mo><msup><mn>10</mn><mrow><mo>-</mo><mn>19</mn></mrow></msup></mrow><msup><mn>10</mn><mrow><mo>-</mo><mn>34</mn></mrow></msup></mfrac><mspace linebreak="newline"></mspace><mo>⇒</mo><msub><mi>v</mi><mn>0</mn></msub><mo>=</mo><mn>1</mn><mo>.</mo><mn>6</mn><mo>×</mo><msup><mn>10</mn><mn>15</mn></msup><mo> </mo><mi>Hz</mi></math></p>

</details>

### Q38. JEE Main 2023 (13 Apr Shift 1)

*Type:* `singleCorrect` | *Level:* `1`

<p>The difference between threshold wavelengths for two metal surfaces <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mi>A</mi></math> and <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mi>B</mi></math> having work function <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><msub><mi>&#981;</mi><mi>A</mi></msub><mo>=</mo><mn>9</mn><mi>eV</mi></math> and <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><msub><mi>&#981;</mi><mi>B</mi></msub><mo>=</mo><mn>4</mn><mo>.</mo><mn>5</mn><mi>eV</mi></math> in <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mi>nm</mi></math> is:</p><p>{Given, <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mi>h</mi><mi>c</mi><mo>=</mo><mn>1242</mn><mo>&#160;</mo><mi>eV</mi><mo>&#160;</mo><mi>nm</mi></math>}</p>

**Options:**

- **(A)** <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mn>540</mn></math>
- **(B)** <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mn>276</mn></math>
- **(C)** <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mn>264</mn></math>
- **(D)** <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mn>138</mn></math>

<details>
<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Correct Option:** **(D) <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mn>138</mn></math>**

**Detailed Solution:**

<blockquote><p>The work function is given by </p><p><math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mfrac><mrow><mi>h</mi><mi>c</mi></mrow><mi>&#955;</mi></mfrac><mo>=</mo><mi>&#981;</mi></math></p><p><math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mo>&#8658;</mo><mi>&#955;</mi><mo>=</mo><mfrac><mrow><mi>h</mi><mi>c</mi></mrow><mi>&#981;</mi></mfrac></math></p><p>By the given data,</p><p><math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><msub><mi>&#955;</mi><mi>A</mi></msub><mo>=</mo><mfrac><mn>1242</mn><mn>9</mn></mfrac><mo>&#160;</mo><mi>nm</mi></math>, <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><msub><mi>&#955;</mi><mi>B</mi></msub><mo>=</mo><mfrac><mn>1242</mn><mrow><mn>4</mn><mo>.</mo><mn>5</mn></mrow></mfrac><mo>&#160;</mo><mi>nm</mi></math>, </p><p>The difference between the wavelengths,</p><p><math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><msub><mi>&#955;</mi><mi>B</mi></msub><mo>-</mo><msub><mi>&#955;</mi><mi>A</mi></msub><mo>=</mo><mfrac><mn>1242</mn><mrow><mn>4</mn><mo>.</mo><mn>5</mn></mrow></mfrac><mo>-</mo><mfrac><mn>1242</mn><mn>9</mn></mfrac><mo>&#160;</mo><mi>nm</mi><mo>=</mo><mfrac><mn>1242</mn><mn>9</mn></mfrac><mo>&#160;</mo><mi>nm</mi></math></p><p><math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mo>=</mo><mn>138</mn><mo>&#160;</mo><mi>nm</mi></math></p></blockquote>

</details>

---

### Q39. JEE Main 2023 (11 Apr Shift 1)

*Type:* `singleCorrect` | *Level:* `2`

A metallic surface is illuminated with radiation of wavelength <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mi>&#955;</mi></math>, the stopping potential is <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><msub><mi>V</mi><mn>0</mn></msub></math>. If the same surface is illuminated with radiation of wavelength <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mn>2</mn><mi>&#955;</mi></math>, the stopping potential becomes <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mfrac><msub><mi>V</mi><mn>0</mn></msub><mn>4</mn></mfrac></math>. The threshold wavelength for this metallic surface will be

**Options:**

- **(A)** <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mn>3</mn><mi>&#955;</mi></math>
- **(B)** <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mn>4</mn><mi>&#955;</mi></math>
- **(C)** <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mfrac><mn>3</mn><mn>2</mn></mfrac><mi>&#955;</mi></math>
- **(D)** <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mfrac><mi>&#955;</mi><mn>4</mn></mfrac></math>

<details>
<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Correct Option:** **(A) <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mn>3</mn><mi>&#955;</mi></math>**

**Detailed Solution:**

<p>Let the threshold frequency be <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><msub><mi>&#955;</mi><mn>0</mn></msub></math>.</p><p>By the equation of photoelectric effect, for wavelength <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mi>&#955;</mi></math>,</p><p><math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mi>e</mi><msub><mi>V</mi><mn>0</mn></msub><mo>=</mo><mi>h</mi><mi>c</mi><mfenced><mrow><mfrac><mn>1</mn><mi>&#955;</mi></mfrac><mo>-</mo><mfrac><mn>1</mn><msub><mi>&#955;</mi><mn>0</mn></msub></mfrac></mrow></mfenced><mo>&#160;</mo><mo>&#160;</mo><mo>&#160;</mo><mo>.</mo><mo>.</mo><mo>.</mo><mo>(</mo><mi>i</mi><mo>)</mo></math></p><p>For the wavelength <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mn>2</mn><mi>&#955;</mi></math>,</p><p><math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mfrac><mrow><mi>e</mi><msub><mi>V</mi><mn>0</mn></msub></mrow><mn>4</mn></mfrac><mo>=</mo><mi>h</mi><mi>c</mi><mfenced><mrow><mfrac><mn>1</mn><mrow><mn>2</mn><mi>&#955;</mi></mrow></mfrac><mo>-</mo><mfrac><mn>1</mn><msub><mi>&#955;</mi><mn>0</mn></msub></mfrac></mrow></mfenced><mo>&#160;</mo><mo>&#160;</mo><mo>&#160;</mo><mo>.</mo><mo>.</mo><mo>.</mo><mo>(</mo><mi>i</mi><mi>i</mi><mo>)</mo></math></p><p>Dividing (i) by (ii)</p><p><math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mn>4</mn><mo>=</mo><mfrac><mrow><mi>h</mi><mi>c</mi><mo>(</mo><mstyle displaystyle="true"><mfrac><mrow><msub><mi>&#955;</mi><mn>0</mn></msub><mo>-</mo><mi>&#955;</mi></mrow><mrow><mi>&#955;</mi><msub><mi>&#955;</mi><mn>0</mn></msub></mrow></mfrac></mstyle><mo>)</mo></mrow><mrow><mi>h</mi><mi>c</mi><mfenced><mstyle displaystyle="true"><mfrac><mrow><msub><mi>&#955;</mi><mn>0</mn></msub><mo>-</mo><mn>2</mn><mi>&#955;</mi></mrow><mrow><mn>2</mn><mi>&#955;</mi><msub><mi>&#955;</mi><mn>0</mn></msub></mrow></mfrac></mstyle></mfenced></mrow></mfrac><mspace linebreak="newline"></mspace><mo>&#8658;</mo><mn>4</mn><mo>=</mo><mfrac><mrow><mn>2</mn><mo>(</mo><msub><mi>&#955;</mi><mn>0</mn></msub><mo>-</mo><mi>&#955;</mi><mo>)</mo></mrow><mrow><msub><mi>&#955;</mi><mn>0</mn></msub><mo>-</mo><mn>2</mn><mi>&#955;</mi></mrow></mfrac><mspace linebreak="newline"></mspace><mo>&#8658;</mo><mn>2</mn><msub><mi>&#955;</mi><mn>0</mn></msub><mo>=</mo><mn>6</mn><mi>&#955;</mi><mspace linebreak="newline"></mspace><mo>&#8658;</mo><msub><mi>&#955;</mi><mn>0</mn></msub><mo>=</mo><mn>3</mn><mi>&#955;</mi></math></p>

</details>

---

### Q40. JEE Main 2023 (10 Apr Shift 2)

*Type:* `singleCorrect` | *Level:* `2`

<p>The variation of stopping potential <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mfenced><msub><mi mathvariant="normal">V</mi><mn>0</mn></msub></mfenced></math> as a function of the frequency <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mo>(</mo><mi>&#957;</mi><mo>)</mo></math> of the incident light for a metal is shown in figure. The work function of the surface is</p><p><img src="https://cdn-question-pool.getmarks.app/pyq/jee_main/10f51844-a374-4e70-a58c-ad8e531cac7d.png" style="width: 300px; height: 250px;" /></p>

**Options:**

- **(A)** <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mn>2</mn><mo>.</mo><mn>98</mn><mo>&#160;</mo><mi>eV</mi></math>
- **(B)** <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mn>2</mn><mo>.</mo><mn>07</mn><mo>&#160;</mo><mi>eV</mi></math>
- **(C)** <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mo>&#160;</mo><mn>1</mn><mo>.</mo><mn>36</mn><mo>&#160;</mo><mi>eV</mi><mo>&#160;</mo></math>
- **(D)** <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mn>18</mn><mo>.</mo><mn>6</mn><mo>&#160;</mo><mi>eV</mi></math>

<details>
<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Correct Option:** **(B) <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mn>2</mn><mo>.</mo><mn>07</mn><mo>&#160;</mo><mi>eV</mi></math>**

**Detailed Solution:**

<blockquote><p>The relation between the stopping potential and the frequency of the incident light is given by</p><p><math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mi>e</mi><msub><mi>V</mi><mn>0</mn></msub><mo>=</mo><mi>h</mi><mi>&#957;</mi><mo>-</mo><mi>&#981;</mi><mo>&#160;</mo><mo>&#160;</mo><mo>&#160;</mo><mo>.</mo><mo>.</mo><mo>.</mo><mfenced><mn>1</mn></mfenced></math></p><p>When <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><msub><mi>V</mi><mrow><mn>0</mn><mo>&#160;</mo></mrow></msub><mo>=</mo><mo>&#160;</mo><mn>0</mn></math>,</p><p><math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mi>&#981;</mi><mo>=</mo><mi>h</mi><mi>&#957;</mi><mo>&#160;</mo><mo>&#160;</mo><mo>&#160;</mo><mo>.</mo><mo>.</mo><mo>.</mo><mfenced><mn>2</mn></mfenced></math></p><p>Substitute the value of the frequency corresponding to zero stopping potential into equation (2) to calculate the required work function of the material.</p><p><math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mtable columnalign="right center left" columnspacing="0px"><mtr><mtd><mi mathvariant="normal">&#981;</mi></mtd><mtd><mo>=</mo></mtd><mtd><mfrac><mrow><mn>6</mn><mo>.</mo><mn>626</mn><mo>&#215;</mo><msup><mn>10</mn><mrow><mo>-</mo><mn>34</mn></mrow></msup><mo>&#160;</mo><mi mathvariant="normal">J</mi><mo>&#183;</mo><mi mathvariant="normal">s</mi><mo>&#215;</mo><mn>5</mn><mo>&#215;</mo><msup><mn>10</mn><mn>14</mn></msup><mo>&#160;</mo><mi>Hz</mi></mrow><mrow><mn>1</mn><mo>.</mo><mn>6</mn><mo>&#215;</mo><msup><mn>10</mn><mrow><mo>-</mo><mn>19</mn></mrow></msup><mo>&#160;</mo><mi mathvariant="normal">J</mi><mo>/</mo><mi>eV</mi></mrow></mfrac></mtd></mtr><mtr><mtd></mtd><mtd><mo>=</mo></mtd><mtd><mfrac><mrow><mn>6</mn><mo>.</mo><mn>626</mn><mo>&#215;</mo><mn>5</mn></mrow><mn>16</mn></mfrac><mo>&#8776;</mo><mn>2</mn><mo>.</mo><mn>07</mn><mi>eV</mi></mtd></mtr></mtable></math></p></blockquote>

</details>
